Of the great variety of the All-Supreme is fully accomplished."

Thus spoke the utterly secret Blessed One Vajrasattva.

"Thus the extensive rite of the ritual of Śrī Paramādya, with its various tales,
Endowed with the experience of unsurpassed accomplishment, the supremely secret music:
Whoever, bearing the lofty banner of variety, constantly relies upon it,
Truly experiences the supreme accomplishment, Great Bliss itself.
Unfailing and supremely endowed with merit,
The rite is supremely eternal;
The great secret of the All-Supreme
Swiftly brings about all accomplishments.
Those who enter the supreme city
With delight and practice there
Attain, through that bliss, the accomplishment
And lordship of utterly supreme desire.
Those who have not attained accomplishment,
And those who are perfecting merit,
If they fully enter the supreme maṇḍala,
These practitioners become fully accomplished here.
If the supreme Vajrasattva
Has not been accomplished through the heart mantra and the rest,
And even when practiced with the near-heart mantra
Remains unaccomplished, entering here, it will be accomplished.
In the root tantra of Śrī Paramādya
It is likewise swiftly accomplished.
Just as, having dwelt in the first house,
The vajra maṇḍala is accomplished,
So, by the accomplishing of all the maṇḍalas,
Not everything is accomplished in that way.
Just as, by seeing the sublime palace
Of the supremely secret,
Even those who have broken their pledges,
Those veiled by every obscuration,
And those touched by grahas such as Māra
Will attain eternal liberation,
So those who have not entered the maṇḍala,
Or who have not accomplished the maṇḍala,
If they enter once more in the supreme way,
Will swiftly become accomplished.
Here there is nothing at all that is not accomplished.
Accomplishment, action and bliss likewise:
All the accomplishments, in fourfold measure,
Should be accomplished through all the rites."

Thus spoke the Blessed One Vajrasattva.

From Śrī Paramādya, the utterly secret rite of the maṇḍala of the glorious great pledge of all the tathāgatas is complete.

Then this is the supreme secret rite of the supreme pledge of the three worlds of all the tathāgatas:

"Buddha, Dharma and Saṅgha:
These are the three great pledges.
On cloth, in the form of supreme bodhisattvas,
One should draw the Three Jewels:
The supreme vajra together with armor,
Well adorned with armor and the rest;
The one who proclaims the vajra weapon;
And the one who holds the lotus and the jewel.
Having fashioned that wheel,
It is the sublime secret maṇḍala.
If one practices it as is fitting,
It will be accomplished threefold."

This is the heart mantra called "the great pledge of all the tathāgatas," which bestows supreme accomplishment without exception: oṃ bhū khaṃ.

"In a house, or else in a grove,
Having adorned it as before,
And having done everything
Through the rite of the supreme weapon, one should recite this:
'Through that very one, the Blessed One,
The bodhisattva and tathāgata,
To benefit those of evil views,
Shows them nirvāṇa.
Striving to tame all without exception,
He has donned firm and certain armor;
Supreme one dwelling fully in existence,
He has donned the armor and the rest.
As Buddha, Dharma and Saṅgha
He has become the Three Jewels,
Supreme maker of the pure glory
Of existence perfectly accomplished.'"

This is the supremely secret vajra of all the tathāgatas.

Then the bell of the supremely secret nature of reality:

"All things have the characteristic of space,
And space too has no characteristic.
Through union equal to space,
The All-Supreme shines forth as sameness."

This is the perfection of wisdom.

Then what is called the vow of the three great pledges:

"When enjoying all enjoyments,
If one partakes of them as one's own deity,
Saying, 'I make the supreme offering to the Three Jewels,'
One makes offering."

This is the accomplishment of the yoga of the Tathāgata.

"Through this rite of skillful means,
If one eats even the five qualities of desire,
Even what is not to be eaten,
One will attain eternal accomplishment."

These are the three great supremely secret pledges of all the tathāgatas.

Having recited this, one should give the heart mantra and have the disciples learn to bind the mudrā, and then accomplish whatever one wishes.

"Then the master, the vajra holder,
Having worshipped with vajra worship,
Through offerings as he wishes
Leads all the disciples in, in the supreme way.
Though one enjoys all enjoyments,
What is offered to the Three Jewels
Through desire, for oneself and for others,
Becomes accomplished as worship itself."

From Śrī Paramādya, the supremely secret rite.

Then the supremely secret rite of the Great Vajra, the king of the wrathful ones:

"On cloth one should draw the Blessed One,
Lord of the entire vajra family,
A vajra blazing like the sun and like fire,
Supremely, according to the rite.
He is seated on a single lotus petal,
Resting in a wheel of his own light.
The ḍākinīs of the great secret vajra yoga
Should be drawn at his sides:
The first, red and white;
The next, white like ash;
The third, white and gazing;
The fourth, stainless like gold.
The accomplished lord of vajra fierceness,
With a vajra khaṭvāṃ raised aloft,
Vajra desire blazing like fire:
At her side place the emblem.
Vajravaiṣṇavī places the vajra wheel,
And likewise the other, the vajra bell or banner.
At her side the one who leans to the left,
Baring her fangs slightly,
Her face laughing, with wrath,
Sets her mind on her own self,
Her body seated facing the corner.
Then its heart mantra is
hūṃ.
Then the near-heart mantra:
Together with vajra, bell and the rest,
Beating music in solitude,
Beating out the song and music of hi,
Fully accomplish the four corners."

This is the rite of recitation here.

"Having shown it in that wheel,
The sublime secret maṇḍala,
Through the rite of the supreme weapon,
Is the full elaboration of all the rites."

Then the giving of the rite of reality, of Dharma and of pledge:

"Wrath arisen from constant passion,
Body that is a mass of vajra fire,
Great blazing Vajrakrodha,
Body that is a host of intense flames,
Great yoga of the blazing vajra:
The ḍākinīs blaze all around."

This is the vajra.

Then the Dharma:

"All things have the characteristic of space,
And space too has no characteristic.
The yogin equal to space
Accomplishes all with utter swiftness.
Through union with the All-Supreme of space,
Declaring all things to be space,
And sealing them with the vajra mudrā,
One devours even the three worlds."

This is the supremely secret pledge of the ḍākinīs of vajra yoga.

"Through this rite, in every way,
One should practice in meditative equipoise.
Through the great accomplishment of the All-Supreme,
The hosts of activities are inexhaustible."

So it is said. Then, having made the dance offering with one's own mudrās through the tathāgata rite, exactly as it is, one will become accomplished.

From Śrī Paramādya, the rite of vajra yoga.

"Then the wheel of ḍākinīs
Of the great accomplished secret yoga
Of the supremely subtle
And utterly excellent vajra is explained.
Having fittingly laid out
The city, the pleasure grove and the rest,
The master of all the vajra holders,
Accomplishing them as prescribed,
Together with the four lovely ones,
Scatters flowers with his own mudrā
And enters the city as is fitting.
Turn the mouth of the left hand upward;
With the great laugh of Vajrarudra,
Through the mudrā of the supreme proclaiming of sound,
Enter and bow to the Protector.
The homage for this is as follows:
Uttering oṃ,
With the breath of utmost joy,"
ru lu ru lu llaṃ six times, with ru lu ru lu llaṃ in between.

This is called "the supreme proclaiming of the sound of homage of Vajrarudra." Here it is the mudrā of the vajra hand. It is called "supremely accomplished" with regard to all mudrās, to all other accomplishments free of delusion and obstruction, and to all activities. The Blessed One Vajrasattva spoke this utterly and supremely secret teaching.

"Then, with the lovely one who holds the pledge,
Having scattered loose flowers,
Through the empowerment of the vajra lotus
One clearly bestows the three pledges."

Here the mudrā of Vajrasattva is this: it is made from the lotus skull cup, and since that is blessed with the vajra, it is called "vajra lotus." Then the binding of the mudrā here:

"All the fingers joined with one another,
Together with the tip of the All-Supreme,
The thumbs covered by the vajra:
This is accomplished as the 'vajra lotus.'"

With this vajra-lotus mudrā one should perform the application of the empowerment of Vajrasattva and empower with the jewel crown of the vajra lotus. Merely through empowerment with this, the tathāgatas reduce to ashes beings who are utterly vicious, fierce, savage and wicked, and one is blessed by them with the name "all the tathāgatas, wheel-turning monarchs possessed of great power over the three realms without exception, victorious over all," and also becomes a supreme and eternal protection. Thus spoke the supremely and utterly secret Blessed One Vajrasattva.

"By that very one the vajra is held,
And the bell of Dharma should be rung.
Having blessed the great mudrā of the pledge,
Recite the heart mantra.
Take hold of the vajra and the rest as obtained:
One is like a second Vajrasattva,
Accomplished as lord of vajra yoga,
And blessed by the Vajra Bearer.
The women too are made to receive the descent,
And through the bringing down of the Teacher's vajra,
Holding emblems together with the vajra,
They fully become yoginīs."

Here, first of all, is the one called "Great Vajra Blazing Like the Sun and Fire, Utterly Reducing to Ashes," the first supreme lord of the king of the wrathful ones, the Great Vajra of all the tathāgatas, who brings about the true descent. Through the skillful means of the great compassion of all the tathāgatas, he holds the vajra of the blazing fire of vengeful wrath, the banner of the khaṭvāṃ together with a vajra, and the bell joined with a pennant. He is the lord of Vajra Pride who brings down utter wrath, with a frown between his brows that is charming yet utterly terrifying, and with yellow eyes that strike terror. With this mudrā of the gaze of the eyes that terrify with the great vajra terror of all the tathāgatas, one accomplishes it by reciting a hundred thousand times. It is able to bring about the passion, summoning, drawing in, subjugation and descent of all the tathāgatas. There is the samādhi of joining all things to space; and merely by uttering it once, all the māras of the three realms without exception, such as Vajrāmṛta, beings who are vicious, fierce, savage and wicked toward all the tathāgatas, all the fierce ḍākinīs, and even the śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas are reduced to ashes; if even they are, what need to mention enemies and others? It is unfailing and unobstructed, and with regard to all accomplishments and all hosts of activities it is supremely accomplished.

Then the recitation of the instruction on this utterly fierce skillful means of great compassion of the Blessed One: with the very subtle breath of the nostrils, through the true vajra word, it is sublimely and supremely accomplished for the hosts of accomplishments of all the activities the mind desires. hūṃ.

Then the great deities, the sovereign ladies of supremely secret vajra yoga, are to be explained.

First of all is the great goddess called "Great Vajra Blazing Fire of Desire." Raising the vajra bow and arrow, her eye glances to the left. With this mudrā of the gaze of the vajra eye she accomplishes everything; merely by sealing with the mudrā of glancing from the corners of her charming, darting eyes, she seals all things; and, unfailing and unobstructed, with regard to all things and all hosts of activities she is supremely accomplished.

Then the recitation of the Vajra Speech Wrathful One, in the voice of a crow's throat, by this Blessed Lady: through the wish of the mind it is supremely accomplished for all accomplishments and all hosts of activities. hrīḥ.

The second is the goddess called "Great Vajra Blazing Fire of Desire, Answer of the Pledge." In the center, praising her own special deity, her face smiling and her small eyes smiling, with this mudrā of the gaze of the vajra eye of great joy she accomplishes everything. Merely by sealing with the mudrā of touch, kissing, embracing and the like, she seals all things; she makes all mudrās hold back; she causes killing through all; and, unfailing and unobstructed, with regard to all accomplishments and all hosts of activities she is supremely accomplished.

Then the recitation of the Vajra Speech Wrathful One, in the voice of a crow's throat, by this Blessed Lady: through the wish of the mind it is supremely accomplished for all accomplishments and all hosts of activities. hūṃ.

The third is the great goddess called "Great Vajra Blazing Fire of Desire, Reducing to Ashes." She holds the great vajra of the Blessed One and the banner of the khaṭvāṃ; with a charming laugh and a frown between her brows, her eyes are wrathful. With this mudrā of the wrathful gaze of ki li ki la, the wrathful one of the Great Vajra, she accomplishes everything. Merely by saying "vajra" and "ru lu ru lu ru lu hni" she conquers all the māras; and, unfailing and unobstructed, with regard to all accomplishments and all hosts of activities she is supremely accomplished. hi.

The fourth is called "Great Vajra Blazing Fire of Desire, Reducing to Ashes, Raiser of the Dead." Her left side in a proud pose, with her right hand she rings the vajra bell on the crown of her own head, and her charming eyes are opened wide. With the mudrā of the gaze of the kindly eye of Vajrarāja she accomplishes everything. By sealing with slaps and the like, with laughter, with striking and with mudrās, she raises all the dead; and, unfailing and unobstructed, with regard to all accomplishments and the hosts of activities she is supremely accomplished.

Then the Vajrakrodha recitation of the bell-sound of this Blessed Mother is supremely accomplished, at the mind's wish, in all accomplishments and in all the hosts of activities. ṣṭa

These and the other boundless supreme ones,
equal to the dharmadhātu and unequaled,
countless as the element of space,
arisen from the Vajra Sun,
fully entered into this maṇḍala
that is the state of all of space,
bring down from every side, without exception,
the lords of the yoga of the supreme vajra.

Then that vajra yogin, the teacher, sets the yogins in their own separate places:

In the form of a crescent-moon maṇḍala
they should be arranged within the maṇḍala.
Having arranged them all, one should accomplish
the sublime accomplishment of the All-Supreme.
Through accomplishing the summoning of all, the great offering,
the blessing of oneself, and the rest,
and having examined and accomplished
that utterly secret maṇḍala,
the master, Vajra Holder of all,
should bring in the vajra disciples.

Here, first of all, the master brings them in. In the assembly maṇḍala, having set the vajrayoginīs in their separate places through the blessing of the three samayas, he should bring in the men as well, and with the awakening mind have them take the samaya of the wrathful one of the Great Vajra and the samaya vows. Having duly placed the boys and the girls, he himself should enter together with the assembly, and perform all the guarding, protecting, and concealing.

Then here is the extensive rite for bringing in the disciples:

Having supremely generated
the unsurpassed awakening mind,
dancing, and offering with palms joined at the crown,
one should bring in all the disciples.
To produce whatever accomplishment is wished,
have the disciple cast a flower.
On whichever yoginī it falls,
that very goddess will be accomplished.
Then, having untied the face covering,
and having received the fee in every way,
through the vajra-lotus empowerment
one should clearly bestow the three samayas.

Here the swearing of the oath and the empowerment are just the same as before. And here the bestowal of the three samayas is this:

Refraining, and discerning each thing,
and the subduing of all the wicked:
the vajra is the very presence of all buddhas,
the awakening mind, great and steadfast.
That weapon all the victors
placed in the hand of Samantabhadra,
so that all the teachings might be accomplished;
therefore he is Vajrapāṇi.

Then the supreme secret of the Vajra Sun Blazing like Fire:

Wrath ever arisen from desire,
Vajrasattva, for the sake of those to be tamed,
is the Vajra Sun blazing like fire,
the one who brings supreme terror.

Then the reality of the supreme secret of the ḍākinīs of the supremely secret vajra yoga:

Arisen from the rising of the Vajra Sun,
this shadow of the tathāgatas,
emanating, drinks the blood
of the wicked, Māra and all the rest, without exception.

This is the reality of the supreme secret.

Then here is the bell that brings down the supremely secret nature of reality:

The exceedingly wicked and fierce,
the sons of the lord Rudra,
and those sunk deep in sleep:
of these the holder of the supreme vajra has spoken.

Thus spoke the Blessed One. With the vajra khaṭvāṅga banner and pennant that bring down the supremely secret nature of reality, it brings down all the bells, brings about full accomplishment, fells all, and raises them up.

Then the samaya of the supreme yoga:

Great blood together with camphor,
mixed with red sandalwood:
having fully entered into the assembly,
the Vajra Holder, with his vajra,
with the tips of the ring finger and the thumb,
endowed with the supreme union of all space,
if he tastes it like the drink of the moon,
will attain everlasting accomplishment.
Enjoying all desirable things,
while indulging in whatever one wishes,
through the union of the All-Supreme of space
one will attain all accomplishments.
Through the union of the All-Supreme of space,
saying, "all of space,"
and sealing it with the vajra mudrā,
one should devour even the three worlds.
Accomplishments, activities, and likewise bliss:
in this there is nothing at all that is not accomplished.
Through the accomplishment of the hosts of activities
one will accomplish every accomplishment without exception.
Unfailing and supremely accomplished,
it subdues all the wicked;
glorious, robbing away all suffering,
this yoga performs every activity.

Thus the Blessed One Vajrasattva spoke this supreme secret.

Then, with flavors and foods, conduct, dance and song,
offerings of music and the like, worship the Protector.
With what has been offered, one should faithfully honor
the great vajra guru, oneself, and others.
Partaking as one wishes
of intoxicating and inebriating drinks,
one should recite, as is fitting, the accompanying recitations
of the Vajra Sun Blazing like Fire and the rest.
With songs, music, and dances
taken up according to one's own secret rite,
with offerings of various mudrās,
with all the vast offerings
of Trailokyavijaya and the others,
together with the hosts of women,
and with enjoyments of whatever is desired,
having made offering, one should fully accomplish the practice.

Thus the Blessed One Vajrasattva spoke this supreme secret.

Then one should give the encouragement of the gathering of all the tathāgatas and the rest:

Equal to the finest particles
of the realms of all beings
is the accomplishment of the king of ritual manuals,
the supreme accomplishment of buddhahood and the rest.
That whose distinctions cannot be apprehended
even by an immeasurable intellect,
the bliss bestowed by the tathāgatas,
is the gathering of all that is sublime.
All that wanes will wane,
all that grows will grow;
by seeing it one becomes invisible;
it makes one fearless from every quarter.
Rudra, Indra, Upendra (Viṣṇu), Candra the Moon,
Sūrya the Sun, and the maṇḍalas of the hosts of ḍākinīs:
enraged, with merely the syllable hūṃ,
one reduces them all to ashes.
Having fully entered and seen
the maṇḍala of the yogic ḍākinīs,
you are utterly freed from every evil,
and all of you abide securely.
Henceforth there is no death for you,
and above all no lower realms.
Others cannot overpower you or kill you;
fear nothing at all, and rejoice!
The accomplishments of utmost existence
are the supreme ones that the Teacher reveals.
This accomplished samaya vow
should be guarded at all times.
All the buddhas have spoken it in accord:
it is the command of the sublime ritual manual.

The awakening mind must not be abandoned,
nor the sublime Dharma, nor likewise the samaya;
and the master must not be disparaged,
for he is the Vajra Holder of all.
For the benefit of all beings,
and so that all the vehicles may be accomplished,
"the pure taming of the wicked":
thus the holder of the supreme vajra has spoken.
The exceedingly wicked and savage,
and the lord's companions and sons as well,
through the fierce rite of mudrā yoga
one should cast into deep sleep.
Always guard your own samaya;
always worship the tathāgatas;
always make offerings to the guru as well:
if offered to him, the fruit is great.
To give to him
is always to give to all the buddhas.
Giving to him is the accumulation of merit,
and from that accumulation comes supreme accomplishment.
Today your birth has borne its fruit,
because you are firmly established in this.
As equal to the king of samayas
you have been blessed by all the buddhas.
Since all the buddhas and Vajra Holders
have empowered you today,
yours is the great kingdom of the three realms:
you are a wheel-turning monarch of power and glory.
Having seen the secret supreme samaya
of the yoginīs of the highest secret of the sublime vajra,
and having eaten the flesh of Māra and the rest,
having attained union with the tathāgatas, you are accomplished.

Thus spoke the Blessed One Vajrasattva.

From the Śrī Paramādya: the rite of the maṇḍala of the ḍākinīs of supremely secret vajra yoga, supremely accomplished.

Then the supremely secret rite of noble Avalokiteśvara for taming beings:

On cloth one should paint the Blessed One,
the principal, Padmanarteśvara, Lord of the Lotus Dance.
With the dances of saṃsāra's magical displays,
a wheel of lightning whirling all around,
paint the hand emblems, the lotus and the rest,
with mudrās formed like a wheel.
On every side of him
one should paint Bhṛkuṭī and the others,
Raudrī and the others in the forms of their hand emblems,
and the lotus goddesses together with lotuses.
When that maṇḍala has been laid out,
that sublime secret maṇḍala,
having done everything with each
of the supreme weapons, one should recite this:

He himself, the buddha, the Blessed One,
is Avalokiteśvara,
who, pure through the means of taming,
performs the dance of play.

This is the supreme vajra.

Just as a red lotus
is not stained by the faults of color
from the various faults of the place where it grows,
so the three realms are pure.

This is the bell of the supremely secret nature of reality.

By whatever rites
beings come to be tamed,
by those very rites the lord of yoga,
pure and supreme as the lotus, should purify them.

This is the Dharma samaya of all the vajras.

Then, as is fitting, one should worship with the offerings of the lotus dance and carry out all the rites in sequence.

Then the rite of the Blessed One Ākāśagarbha for accomplishing all aims:

Having drawn the disk of the sun,
on that painted cloth one of great intellect
should paint Ākāśagarbha,
green, like beryl.
In one of his hands rests a lotus.
Set within the wheel of his light are
Empowerment and Treasure,
Supreme Victor, Sovereign, and Great Offering.
Having laid out the wheel
of that sublime secret maṇḍala,
with the application of the supreme weapons,
the extensive rite is as before.

Then the supreme vajra:

This sun is sublime;
it beholds all the tathāgatas.
The Blessed One Ākāśagarbha
fulfills every wish.

Then the bell of the Dharma jewel:

Of all fulfillments of wishes,
fulfillment of the wish for wealth is supreme.
And since that too is fully accomplished at will,
freedom from desire is great bliss.

What is not to be given should be given:
the blisses of every desire,
to oneself and to others,
so that every wish may be fulfilled.

This is the supreme samaya.

Through this samaya, supremely,
O Vajrasattva, Great Bliss,
swiftly bring about the accomplishment
of the truly sublime, the supreme, the unchanging.
Thus the ways of the supreme secret
are the rites, supremely extensive, as they are;
that great supreme one, the essence of all the ritual manuals,
brings about the special accomplishment.

From the Śrī Paramādya: the rite for accomplishing all the ritual manuals.

Then, the supreme practice of all the great accomplishments,
Great Bliss,
the great mudrā of Vajrasattva,
so that the ritual manual of the All-Supreme may be accomplished:
for beings who are not fit vessels,
who commit every evil,
the great being has acted for beings' sake
through seeing and the rest.
For those blind ones who do not see,
who bear the signs of lacking merit and the like,
the causes that are the means of seeing
Vajrasattva and the other supreme beings
are the binding of the supreme samaya
by the vajra of body, speech, and mind
of Vajrasattva and the other supreme beings:
a samaya hard to transgress.
Because the stable bodies
of Vajrasattva and the other beings
are known as their mudrās,
they emanate them for the sake of all accomplishments.
By merely holding these,
Vajrasattva and the other supreme ones
are seen, are directly seen,
and are supremely gathered in.
Because by merely binding the mudrā
they are gathered in,
Vajrasattva and the other beings,
fearing its breach, come to be accomplished.
Just as the seal that marks
the command of kings is hard to transgress,
no one at all is able
to transgress a clear seal.
Buddhahood itself, or the state of Vajra Holder,
is not attained, even in ten million eons,
as swiftly as it is
through holding the supreme mudrā.
In accord with the world that is to be trained,
accomplishments have been taught.
Their means is the mudrā,
which renders all accomplishments supreme.
By merely binding it,
the assembled ones of great bliss,
Vajrasattva and the others, in an instant
make the blessing firm.

So it is said.

Then, having spoken this, the Blessed One brought about a full awakening through the descents of the vajra of the Blessed One. That vajra is skillful and supremely subtle, profound and vast, inconceivable, supremely pure, without beginning or end, inexhaustible and unending, unfailing and unobstructed. It is the great bliss of utterly supreme joy, entire, and mental happiness, entire. It is the great sovereign of the great accomplishment of all the realms of beings, without exception and with none left out, the supreme of all supremely unchanging accomplishments. He then blessed this supreme weapon called "The Supreme Secret of Amogha, the Vajra Secret of Great Bliss," the supreme of the Later Tantra, the supremely secret rite.

"Next I shall explain the rite of the single practice of blessing oneself by the samaya of all the tathāgatas." Thus spoke the Blessed One Vajrasattva.

Each day, first of all, once,
recite the great mudrā and the heart mantra.
Through the single self-blessing
in which the mudrās of all are perfected, one is accomplished.

This is supreme accomplishment.

Then the supremely accomplished rite of practicing the single, supremely secret self-blessing:

Once, in the supreme threefold samaya,
in the chamber of the sublime secret,
having obtained and taken up the vajra and the rest,
one will be blessed by the Vajra Bearer.

Then the thoroughly accomplished, extensive special rite of practicing the single, utterly and supremely secret self-blessing:

Vajra and bell, the nature of all,
I am endowed with a body of various mudrās;
I, the yogin of all the mudrās,
become Vajrasattva himself.
Through this rite of accomplishment
Vajrasattva is truly gathered in,
and one is blessed as the great sovereign
of the great accomplishment of the All-Supreme.
Unfailingly, oneself is fully blessed,
by its very nature, in every respect.
In short, all the mudrās
are accomplished by binding them oneself.
Because of the self-blessing, and because it is unfailing,
accomplishments come swiftly and without effort;
by practicing with the hosts of activities,
all accomplishments are swiftly achieved.
Through this there is nothing at all that is not accomplished:
accomplishments, activities, and likewise bliss.
By practicing the blessing of all,
one accomplishes everything, the unchanging.

Thus spoke the Blessed One Vajrasattva.

From the Śrī Paramādya: the rite of blessing all accomplishments, called "The Supreme Secret."

"Next I shall explain the rite of all the supremely secret yogas." Thus spoke the Blessed One Vajrasattva.

Here, first of all, for the sake of its meaning, comes the verbal root of "yoga." "This is exertion": that is the meaning of the verbal root. If one asks, "Exertion in what?" it is said: it is yoga because one applies oneself to recollecting one's own chosen deity. Further, the other terms "perfect gathering," "perfect complete application," and "perfect union" are synonyms that express yoga. If one asks what perfect complete application is: because one follows the practice of the yoga of recollecting one's own chosen deity, it is called the application of practicing the yoga of one's own chosen deity.

If one asks how it is the yoga of all: the samaya yoga is what is called the yoga of all.

Since even all the tathāgatas
do not transgress it,
the mudrā is proclaimed as samaya,
because the form in the mind is made stable.

This is the meaning of samaya. The yoga of following that practice is the samaya yoga, and it is called the yoga of all. Just as what is called a compound comes about by combining many substances, so, because it is the perfect complete application of the application of the mudrā of the nature of all, the yoga of all is the samaya yoga.

If one asks how it is the yoga of the nature of all:

The perfect union with the self of all the buddhas,
endowed with their shapes and bodies just as they are,
one should set within one's very self,
and then follow the practice.

This is one's own samaya yoga, which is called the yoga of all. Its following practice is:

Whatever mudrā
each chosen deity binds,
through the yoga of that very deity
one should apply that very mudrā.

So it is said.

Then the supremely and thoroughly accomplished extensive rite of following the practice of the most supreme of the supremely secret:

I am all the buddhas,
and I am also the Radiant One.
Therefore, through the yoga of one's own deity,
one should contemplate oneself.

This is called the supremely secret great yoga of all the mudrās.

Then the utterly and supremely accomplished extensive special rite of following the practice of the supreme of the utterly and supremely secret:

In accord with union with one's own deity,
endowed with the empowerment of the various mudrās,
the lord of all the mudrās, the lord of yoga,
becomes supremely accomplished in the unchanging.

The yoga of the supreme samaya of the variety of all the ritual manuals is called perfect complete application.

Then, further, what is called even more utterly and supremely accomplished than the supremely accomplished extensive special rite of following the practice of the supreme of the utterly and supremely secret:

Joining oneself with Vajrasattva,
endowed with his voice and body just as they are,
the lord of all, the lord of the yoga of mudrā,
will accomplish the supreme unchanging.
This, following the clear samaya
of all the buddhas,
is the accomplishment of the lord of all yogas:
clear, and the utterly supreme accomplishment.
Through this there is nothing at all that is not accomplished:
accomplishments, activities, and likewise bliss;
the accomplishment of the hosts of activities
and every accomplishment without exception are swiftly achieved.

This is the yoga of the secret samaya, utterly, supremely, and thoroughly accomplished.

Enjoying all desirable things,
and indulging in whatever one wishes,
with these yogas
one should fully worship one's own self.

This is the utterly secret, unsurpassed yoga of the great samaya of the Great Vehicle of all the tathāgatas. It is the yoga of the own samaya of the glory of all the tathāgatas, the samaya yoga of the joy of glorious Vajra Amogha, the glory of great joy. It is the king of the yogas of the accomplishment of the supreme elixir, unsurpassed among all. It is the Tathāgata, the Blessed One, glorious Vajrasattva.

It is the sublime, supreme accomplishment in all of supreme Vajrasattva's life and youthfulness, freedom from illness, form and complexion, splendor, might, strength, and vigor, all the supreme accomplishments, glory, good fortune, great joy, utter bliss, and mental happiness. To the full extent, entirely and with nothing left out, it supremely accomplishes the hosts of activities of all accomplishments, such as the state of great sovereign over all the sublime great accomplishments without exception. Merely by sealing, it is fully accomplished in an instant.

If one asks for what reason, it is this:

Conduct depends upon the mudrā;
the mudrā rests upon yoga;
yoga is to be practiced in its special forms;
yoga accomplishes through the samaya.
The mind is empty, like an illusion;
apart from the body it is not established.
The body too is like a wall,
without activity when bereft of mind.
A body bereft of mind,
and a mind bereft of body,
are not established; and likewise this
is declared in the rite of mudrā and yoga.
Just as the body is accomplished by the mind,
and the mind too by the body,
so it is with the mudrā through yoga,
and with yoga, accomplished through the samaya.
Thus, because samaya and yoga
accomplish one another,
being mutually and always of one nature,
one comes to be accomplished as a tathāgata.

Thus the Blessed One Vajrasattva spoke this utterly and supremely secret, supreme accomplishment.

If one asks how all the mudrās and the yogas are followed in practice:

Because the mudrā is the body made stable,
it is made stable by bliss;
by suffering it is shaken,
or else it meets with death.
Yoga arises from the mind;
it is perfected through mental happiness;
by unhappiness it is shaken,
or else it comes to cessation.
Therefore, with every effort,
follow the practice of mudrā and yoga.
Without austerities and without fixed restrictions,
one should practice with bliss and joy.

Thus the Blessed One Vajrasattva spoke this utterly supreme secret.

If one asks what the bliss of following the practice of the yoga of all the samayas is:

Enjoying all desirable things,
and indulging in whatever one wishes,
through the yoga of one's own deity
one should worship oneself and others.

So it is said. This is the yoga of the still greater Great Vehicle of perfect awakening, the unsurpassed Great Vehicle, the supreme secret of all the tathāgatas, called "The Blessed One Vajrasattva, Supreme Bliss."

If one asks what the following practice of the yoga of all the samayas is:

Through the unbearable hardships of austerity,
one afflicted with suffering is swiftly withered;
through suffering the mind is distracted
and is not applied to yoga.
Therefore, enjoying whatever one wishes,
eating everything, and likewise doing everything,
engaging in whatever acts one wishes,
one practices the conduct as one wishes.
Wherever one walks,
or laughs, or speaks,
wherever it may be, it is just so.
Even one who has not entered the maṇḍala,
or who still has obscurations,
as a yogin of one's own deity
becomes so even through words.
Through this very yoga
one should accomplish everything.
No evil conduct or wrongdoing whatever
will harm one in any way.
Great desire, the nature of all,
the sublime sovereignty, will be accomplished:
the king of all the Vajra Holders,
the All-Supreme, the sublime sovereign.
If buddhahood and the state of a bodhisattva
are easily found through this yoga,
what need is there to speak of the rest,
of accomplishments and the hosts of supreme activities?
Unfailing and supremely accomplished,
it brings about the following practice of all the mudrās.
This glorious yoga, which robs away all suffering,
is the supreme unchanging.

Thus the Blessed One Vajrasattva spoke this supreme mental happiness.

If one asks how all yoga is bound:

Without thinking, one speaks of everything;
and likewise, even having thought of everything,
absorbed in wisdom alone,
one is absorbed in its very nature.

If one asks how the binding and the explanation of all yoga are:

Because through union with one's own deity
one is fully joined with all accomplishments,
this union with wisdom alone
is here called yoga.

This is called the supremely and utterly secret rite of the yoga of all the mudrās.

Then the Blessed One spoke the binding and the explanation of all-yoga, great yoga, and utmost yoga:

Through joining oneself with Vajrasattva,
and joining in turn with all the offerings,
this full joining with all the yogas
should be called yoga.
Of it this should be said:
this great reality is accomplished;
its nature, supreme among the supreme,
is utterly and supremely secret,
and it is not to be told to ordinary people.
The vajra master should apply the rite of all-yoga
to a supreme disciple,
one who follows that reality with faith;
to the others, in stages.
In this there is nothing at all that is not accomplished:
accomplishments, activities, and bliss.
By following the practice of all-yoga,
one accomplishes everything unchanging.
Whoever has even this one
clear rite of yoga
cannot be overpowered or slain by others,
and will not be overcome by Māra and the rest.
That one will be fully accomplished
as the king, the Vajra Holder,
Vajrasattva, the Tathāgata,
the lord of the yoga of the great accomplishment of the All-Supreme.

Thus spoke the Blessed One Vajrasattva.

From the Śrī Paramādya: the supremely secret rite of all the utterly secret yogas.

"Next I shall explain the supremely accomplished tantra of the mudrās. It fully accomplishes all accomplishments of all the ritual manuals, beginning with the supreme accomplishment, and these are unfailing and unobstructed, supremely skillful, subtle, vast and profound, inconceivable and utterly immeasurable, entire and with nothing left out." Thus the Blessed One Vajrasattva spoke the wisdom tantra of all the mudrās.

With the lotus feet placed
at ease upon the left lap,
binding the cross-legged posture of the sattva,
one holds one's own body upright.
By brandishing the vajra one is victorious over all.
For the sake of all supreme accomplishments,
through the vajra of body, speech, and mind,
one becomes Vajrasattva himself.
Through this, Vajrasattva,
or buddhahood itself, radiance itself,
the sovereign of all accomplishments,
will be accomplished in this very life.

Thus spoke the Blessed One Vajrasattva, the stable nature of all.

Even if one has not entered the maṇḍala,
one who has fully taken up the great mudrā
will be worshiped and honored with mudrās
even by the sugatas.
Whoever sees this one and bows,
and offers praise and worship,
sees the Teacher and bows to him,
and thereby worships the Lord.
The great sovereign of the perfect bliss
of the great passion of all accomplishments
is accomplished through the great mudrā,
like a second Vajrasattva.
All accomplishments, those of the gods and the rest,
and the bliss that comes of all activities,
through the blessing of the great mudrā
enter in, especially.
Through it there is nothing at all that is not accomplished:
accomplishments, activities, and likewise bliss.
Blessed by the great mudrā,
by the power of practice everything supreme arises.

Then: the mudrā of the great samaya of all the supreme practices. It embraces the samaya, the Dharma, and the activity of all the great maṇḍalas of all the great beings, Vajrasattva and the rest, who are without beginning or end, and all the practices of summoning, causing to enter, bringing down, attracting with passion, and subjugating, without fail. It is called "The Samaya of the Vajra Secret of Great Bliss." Its binding is this:

Joining all the fingers evenly
is called the vajra palms-joined.
Binding all the fingertips inward
is explained as the vajra bond.
Within the vajra bond,
join the index fingers and the thumbs.
With the right thumb as the sign,
bend the tip and press.
Having bound the five arrows gathered together,
the middle fingers rest within the bond;
thumbs and little fingers are bound as a vajra:
this is its first, supreme vajra.
Having bound that vajra bond,
with the two middle fingers as vajra hooks,
in that very manner of summoning
strike your own heart with the hook.
With this king of hooks,
Vajrasattva, the great king,
is summoned by the hook of the vajra of joy.
Then again, practice the near-heart mantra.
Struck fiercely at the heart,
he is tormented by the fire of passion;
his heart stolen, overpowered and delighted,
he swiftly comes, and accomplishment is won.

Thus spoke the Blessed One, Great Passion and Great Bliss, the Great Vajra, the Blessed One, the great Vajradhara.

Likewise, when sealed, all the tathāgatas, the bodhisattva vajras and the rest, and all things stationary and moving, come and are swiftly accomplished. If they do not come, they will be destroyed. This is the mudrā of the supreme heart called "The Samaya of Great Bliss, Vajra Amogha."

The vajra water offering: the vajra palms-joined, with the palms fully extended.

Then, with the first vajra mudrā, the middle fingers as a vajra holding a flower stalk, one should recite. If one empowers with vajra water and the vajra fist, it serves for all accomplishments, such as lordship over all and the increase of life; and if one drinks it, it destroys all suffering.

Then the mudrā for entering the first maṇḍala:

If one binds the three pennants and the khaṭvāṅga,
one rejoices as far as one is able.
Releasing it, circling in the vajra dance,
the great one holds it, circling all around.
The joining in the form of a kaputa
is placed upon the crown and lowered;
then, gracefully releasing the index fingers, the little fingers and the rest,
one offers it to the Vajra Bearer.
Through this, even without entering the maṇḍala,
one attains the state of a vajra master,
because by merely entering oneself
the offering is unfailingly accomplished.
Whether complete or incomplete,
or else, once again, however many
flowers and other offerings are signified,
the mudrā that grants all common accomplishments
is the supreme three pennants and khaṭvāṅga:
gracefully releasing the last fingers and the rest,
whirling supremely like flashing lightning,
that is explained as the vajra dance.
Lāsyā, the joyful vajra one,
is worshiped with the offering of the vajra dance.

By bowing with smiling, graceful eyes, one enamors Vajrasattva and all the tathāgatas, and causes them to bow in return. Circling the vajra palms-joined over all, without exception: this is the mudrā of praising all.

The supreme hook of all the tathāgatas is this. Form the vajra bond with the thumbs extended, seated cross-legged; the two index fingers are bent, the middle fingers even, with an opening like a bud. This supreme limb is placed upon the crown. Then, binding the diadem all around on the right side, in front one makes the gesture of tying a knot with the two vajra fists. Having made the binding of the garland, gracefully releasing the little fingers and the rest, one makes the gesture of tying silk streamers on both sides, and seals with evenly joined palms. By this one receives the empowerment of Vajrasattva.

Then, empowered with the water of the vajra nectar of great bliss, one becomes the vajra master of all and the great Vajradhara. With the vajra fist one should bestow a five-pronged vajra, red in color, its prongs blazing with vajra light. The disciple, too, should offer the fee to the master, and should then receive all the empowerments, of the flower and the rest, from the master of all, with the mudrā of homage made by circling the vajra palms-joined. Such is the rite of all the maṇḍalas.

Then the mudrā tantra for practicing the king, the great Vajradhara, accomplished as sovereign of all accomplishments. With the hook of the great secret of great bliss, by reciting one's own heart mantra once, one invites Vajrasattva. Seated in the cross-legged posture of the sattva, one presents the offering of the vajra dance, a dance like flashing lightning. Holding the vajra fist, one proudly raises the vajra weapon and the rest, and bows with Lāsyā, the joyful vajra one.

Singing songs with vajra song,
reciting for whatever aim one pleases,
and worshiping properly with the offering of vajra dance,
one swiftly attains accomplishment.

Then this secret song of the primal vajra should be sung, beginning with the first line and so on:

Mind passionately attached to the supreme bliss of all,
Vajrasattva, you are utterly joyful.
May I become great bliss, steadfast and outpouring.
Discerning each thing, swiftly accomplishing, I pay homage.

With this very vajra song
one praises Great Bliss once.
Impassioned, he is deeply delighted,
and, having become steadfast, he brings accomplishment.

With the great mudrā one should bless oneself once by means of the heart mantra.

With the tip of the tongue utterly unmoving,
and the teeth and both lips well joined,
vajra speech, free of melody,
brings all the families to full accomplishment.

The ribita and other songs of hūṃ,
ornamented, as one wishes and as one is able,
the six-note and other sweet songs,
and songs of grāma and rāga as one wishes:
with the vajra dance of utmost joy
one should sing the supreme gandharva song.

This speech is supremely accomplished: among all recitations, it is the recitation of whatever is needed, as much as one desires. The aim of recitation is to know the near-heart mantra; it is the practice of Vajrasattva. Having blessed oneself with the great mudrā, gazing likewise with the vajra gaze, holding with Vajra Pride, one should accomplish the Blessed One by the syllable hūṃ and the recitation of song. Accomplishing at once is done through the recitation of what one desires, just as it is.

Then, after that, according to one's diligence, as one wishes, as one desires, and as one has been blessed, through the sung recitation of the syllable hūṃ, by the force of the joy of Vajra Amogha, the great joy, one accomplishes all things, Vajrasattva and the rest, just as one wishes and as gives delight.

Next, the great mudrā:
by the vajra of body, speech, and mind,
following the form just as it is,
fully endowed with the mudrā of the sign,
one becomes the great being oneself.

Next, the samaya mudrā is the vajra of great bliss, as explained. From it arise the vajra arrow and the rest. Namely: simply bending the two index fingers, facing outward, is the vajra arrow. Binding the thumbs together is the vajra ki li ki la. Opening the two thumbs, with the tips of the two index fingers, the middle fingers not intertwined, and the ring and little fingers level and opened, is the vajra makara. Circling and resting on the two thighs is Vajrakāmeśvarī. Circling the vajra palms at one's own heart, gazing with flirtatious eyes, bowing with the head, placing them on the crown and lowering them; scattering upward, pouring downward, joining the thumbs and circling, and stretching out fully and anointing: these are declared to be the vajra offerings that present flowers, incense, lamps, and scent.

In the outer corners: circling the vajra palms at the heart is flirtation. Circling them around the mouth and placing them on the crown. With the left hand, its index finger bent, hooked by the right, one plays the vajra lute; this is the praise of vajra song. Dancing with the two vajra fists, and binding the vajra palms at the crown. In the vajra bond, bound inward, the right index finger is Aṅkuśī, the hook. With the two thumbs, the knot. The knot of the index fingers and thumbs. Binding the thumbs within the two.

Next, the secret samayas: the secret hook of the great bliss vajra samaya. What arises from it is this:

Joining thumb and index tip, the tips of the middle fingers
are made like eyeballs, holding.
Beneath the vajra of the middle fingers
one should press and squeeze with the thumbs.
Like the vajra guide,
the ring and little fingers opened,
circled and placed on the knees.
All the others are as explained.

Lowering the samaya vajra: if the two index fingers are bent in two, it is the vajra fist.

Next, the karma and samaya mudrās are made with the two fists holding vajras. Namely: the left in pride, the right held at the heart in the manner of brandishing: this is Vajra Pride. Holding at the heart with brandishing. The manner of brandishing the vajra is ki li ki la. Raising the banner. Brandishing both in pride is the vajra sovereign lady of all desires. In the corners, the vajra dancer is as explained. Lāsyā and the others are likewise.

For the vajra gatekeepers: with the knot of the left, the right hand coming above it, one gives the vajra command. With that, the right is brandished. Holding the index fingers and thumbs at the wrists, one knots them and squeezes. It is in the manner of ringing a bell at one's own heart. It is also said: "By these one should summon, bind, and cause all to enter, and accomplish them unfailingly."

These signs of supreme accomplishment, done properly,
swiftly bestow the great accomplishment.
Through these virtuous primal mudrās
even those without a maṇḍala succeed.

Next, the binding of the great mudrā that accomplishes even for one without accomplishment: it should be practiced in the manner of a garland of the four mudrās of the primal vajra. Causing to enter, summoning, making steadfast, and the activities are done by gaze. One should place it at the sign, the navel, the heart, the place of empowerment, and the crown. Placing the middle-finger vajra at the heart, the sign, the mouth, and the place of empowerment, one should drink. The vajras of the index, middle, ring, and little fingers. Brandishing at the shoulders, the heart, the tongue, and the crown. Producing in the sky, the index and ring fingers blazing, joy at the heart, and circling around one's own body. Having circled all, one should place it on all the sense faculties. The middle-finger vajra touches all the sense faculties, by performing its own action. Placing it at the place of empowerment, it likewise brings accomplishment. It is done by placing it on the ground. Circling everywhere, one should emanate with the mind. Unsmiling, wrathful, blazing, and moving: thus one gazes with the gaze. Eating, drinking, moving the hips, and commanding with the crown. Circling everywhere, and with mindfulness everywhere. The four vajras of the little fingers, thumbs, and middle fingers are made at the heart. With the two index fingers, together with the syllable hūṃ, one should summon. With the middle-finger vajra one makes an eye horizontally on the forehead.

Binding it at one's own heart, gazing beneath the earth with an unmoving, unblinking gaze, one should say ha hūṃ dhaṃ. Having drawn the faces of yakṣas and the like, one summons them with the vajra. If it is placed in the lower direction, they become constant. If it is placed in the middle of the mouth, they fully enter. If one bows, they make offering. By the heart they are accomplished. Tearing all the fingers apart at once is the action of mind. Having bound it: jaḥ hūṃ baṃ hoḥ. Binding it with the middle fingers outside, one circles the crown, the heart, the mouth, and the directions. Placing it at the heart, one meditates on it as one's own vajra. One meditates on oneself as a bodhisattva. One recollects oneself as the Tathāgata. One recollects oneself as the great Vajradhara.

Next, the mudrā tantra for accomplishing the sets of rites. The mudrās arising from the vajra dance are unfailing and unobstructed even by me, Vajradhara, and by all the tathāgatas. The rite of the uṣṇīṣa of the supreme vajra body: squeezing the vajra bond, the jewel of the middle fingers, circling, the vajra of all the fingers, and the wheel of light rays of the remaining fingers. With the two wrathful vajra fists one turns the wheel of the directions, circling. Circling at the mouth. By lowering it to the heart and making it blaze, there is in all directions a fence of blazing vajras, which I too, the great wrathful one, the blazing vajra, am able to enter. One should declare the samaya. One undertakes the practice.

By the seizings of Vajrasattva's vajra gaze, the third-day, the fourth-day, the recurring, the constant, and the immeasurable are fully pacified, with an eye that circles and does not blink. The rite with the two vajra fists, the hook-knot of the little fingers, and the two index fingers level: it is placed firmly at the heart. This is the action. One should press down with the tips of the index fingers. Laughing, with a beaming face. Having circled, one should likewise place it at the ūrṇā between the brows. Binding the vajra with two khaṭvāṅgas, the little fingers in the manner of perfecting the vajra, it is increased. This is the way of the condensed meaning. Lifting with the vajra. Snatching with the palms. Striking with the vajra.

In the vajra bond, the two index fingers bent, made level and placed inside, with the two thumbs opened as fangs: this is the mudrā of Vajrayakṣa. With it, striking the palm on the forehead. Rubbing the nails of the index fingers. Showing the middle-finger vajra. Having done so, one swiftly releases the mudrā. Joining the fingers is the vajra uṣṇīṣa.

Two tripatāka hands, one facing upward and one facing downward, making a knot of the index and middle fingers and bringing those index and middle fingers together: this is the rite of Vajrahūṃkāra. Squeezing the knot. Releasing the mudrā all at once. Squeezing, then tearing apart. Striking with the middle fingers, in the manner of falling from the sky. Also in the manner of raising up. The two tripatāka hands level, the tips of the little fingers forming a vajra, the middle fingers encircled by the knot, one places the tips of the index fingers at the place of empowerment.

Splitting is done with the syllable hūṃ. Freeing from grahas is done with the syllables hūṃ mu. Raising from death is done with the syllable a. Increasing life is done with the song of the syllable hūṃ. Perfect protection is done with the Dharma speech of the syllable haṃ.

Having bound the great lotus, one recites as much as one desires. Or else, with the lotus fist, the left in pride, a fire offering of butter, sesame oil, sesame, rice, and mineral powder is supremely effective: oṃ ni bar ṇa sarba dharma tā bajra ha ni haṃ oṃ bi ra jo bi ga ta sarba dha rma bajra ha ri ṇi haṃ oṃ saṃ no pas rba dharma pra ti bi dha bajra ha ri ṇi haṃ oṃ ni ri ti mi ta sarba dha rma tā bajra ha ra ṇi haṃ oṃ sva bhā ba bha sa ra sarba dha rma ha ri ṇi haṃ

With two ka ta ka hands, in the manner of perfecting the vajra bond, rubbing the nails. Moving the vajra bond downward and upward. Releasing the little fingers and the rest of the vajra bond is dance. The index-finger vajra of the vajra bond brings forth speech. By the samaya hook of Great Bliss, Vajra Amogha, one clearly displays Vajrasattva and the others in all things, stable and moving, in the manner of reflections, and in the manner of echoes one causes all sentient beings and oneself to hear the truth.

Binding the two fists of Vajrakrodha, the rite of the vajra gaze. Namely, giving an unsmiling gaze. Rolling the eyeballs upward. By closing them, the target swiftly falls. Raising from below. Also sweeping. Binding the two vajra fists, bending the index fingers and circling, with a terrifying face, fangs bared, one brings about immobility. All the limbs go slack. One falls into deep sleep with mouth agape. Striking at one's own heart. Swift tasting.

The rite of raising up from below: binding the two vajra fists, the mouth agape, having a terrifying mouth, with a crown whose hair stands on end, it is done with the syllable bho. Pressing with the vajra fist, the threatening forefinger at the head together with the syllable hūṃ, and oneself swiftly enraged: this causes falling.

The mudrā of Vajrayakṣa, with the ring and little fingers level and opened as a banner, and the rite of the armlet of the supreme victory banner: bowing, laughing, lolling the tongue, brandishing the two shoulders in pride, and raising the victory banner.

Seated in the sattva cross-legged posture, by brandishing the vajra itself: blowing breath into the sky, and with a gaze that moves all about and closes, this is the way to bring down rain. And with the recitation of the wrathful Vajra Sun Blazing like Fire, stealing the sight, circling the limbs with cold, and making everything blaze intensely, one brings it down.

In the vajra bond, this is Vajra Mindfulness. In the vajra bond, the left index finger bent as the stick and gathered by the right index finger is the vajra lute. Rubbing the palms of the vajra bond. The middle fingers level like a bud form the tongue. This is the mudrā of the vajra of utmost joy. The rite of the great bliss vajra offering is the recitation of the primal song, as much as one desires.

Unmoving, laughing, and summoning are done by Aṅkuśī of the vajra gaze of utmost joy. Holding one's own lower lip in with the teeth. Moving one's own organ. Through the vajra dance of utmost joy there is also the command one desires in the mind: if even the Blessed One Vajrasattva, when it is applied to him, cannot transgress it, what need is there to speak of other things, stable or moving?

The two thumbs of the vajra palms, in the sattva cross-legged posture, with the index fingers raised: this is the supreme body of the Blessed One Vajrasattva. By holding it for just a single instant one is thoroughly blessed, and having held and worshiped the Blessed One, one accomplishes him; it brings success and serves every purpose among all the accomplishments. Placing it on the crown, one should circle it clockwise above. Placing it on the ground, one should draw it upward with a wrathful frown. If one draws an image of a yakṣa and places it at its heart, it becomes a servant. At a shrine of Maheśvara (Śiva), one should recite wrathfully. At a shrine of the mātṛkās, one should add the syllable bhyo to the binding; then all the mātṛkās with their retinues will render service and carry out every command, such as making music and the like.

With the mudrā of brandishing the vajra and a laughing face joined with wrath, the rite is to be done in this way: when they have assembled, one should accomplish it pressing down with the left foot. One should evoke a maiden in front and apply the two eyes to captivate her. Having drawn a vulva (bhaga), one should gaze at it and practice. Pressing Maheśvara down with the left foot, one should apply the syllable hūṃ. Piercing Kāmeśvara, the lord of desire, with the vajra arrow, one then places it facing downward and brandishes it. This is the explanation called the samaya of great bliss through the secret vajra offering.

Making everything vajra: with the two vajra fists one binds on the armor, placing them two by two at the crown and at the place of empowerment, two at the two sides, and likewise binding silk streamers in the manner of a garland. Shooting the vajra arrow to the left. The Vajra Proud One; also the great mudrā.

This is the extensive rite of the tantra of all mudrās, from the Śrī Paramādya.

Next, the mudrā tantra of all the tathāgatas: it is Sattvavajrī. The fist of all the tathāgatas. When empowerment has been conferred with Vajraratna, one should give the five-pronged vajra with its light rays. The offerings are made with the level palms, by scattering upward and the rest, as appropriate.

Binding the two tathāgata fists: the left index finger, facing outward, is grasped by the right fist facing it. This is called the fist of all the tathāgatas of supreme awakening, the perfect awakening of all the tathāgatas. By binding this unfailing and unobstructed mudrā, one becomes irreversible from unsurpassed, perfect and complete awakening, endowed with its fruit, and cannot be overcome by Māra or others. Whoever binds this mudrā realizes the very state of a tathāgata. To worship such a one is to worship all the tathāgatas. Herein is the extensive rite of all the great mudrās.

The samaya mudrā is the great lotus. The vajra mudrā. It brings realization as all the tathāgatas. To worship such a one is to worship all the tathāgatas. The supreme body has been explained. The vajra karma mudrā is explained.

The vajra palms stretched downward, the tips of thumbs and index fingers bound, raised from the heart, with the tips placed firmly at the place of empowerment: this is Ratnavajrī. That same, with the two upward-facing index fingers bent outward, is the vajra lotus. Sattvavajrī is the binding of the middle fingers outside. The Dharma mudrā.

Next, the karma mudrā is explained as the vajra fist. The left hand, together with the robe, is held at one's own heart in the manner of raising, and the right presses the earth: this is called the Complete Victory Maker, and by it the forces of Māra are crushed. That same, turned around and held facing upward, is called Exalting: it raises up from downfalls and bestows prophecy. That same, opening a lotus petal at the edge of the robe, is the source of phenomena (dharmodaya): it purifies all phenomena. That same, held as the tripatāka, is the raising one: it bestows accomplishment without doubt. These perform their actions with the right hand, are unfailing, and succeed merely by being bound. The rest are done by holding them as appropriate.

Next, the karma mudrās: the fist of all the tathāgatas torments with illness. Purifying the eyes. Placing at the heart. Sweeping. Snatching with the fist. Eating. Enraged, one should scatter. Drawing out the kīla. Striking the heel. Having written the syllable oṃ, one should erase it. One should seize with the teeth. One should strike, together with the syllable hūṃ. Having drawn a weapon, one should erase it. One should perform fire offerings everywhere. One should perform a fire offering of dūrvā grass. Placing it on the crown, one should act by the gaze. Holding a khaṭvāṅga above. One should circle it in one's own house. One should place it in one's own house. One should cause it to enter. One should draw it out from the heart. One should circle it around the body. Bringing forth speech. One should squeeze it at the heart.

The great mudrā of all the tathāgatas is the supreme body. One should make it firm. One should place it on the sense faculties. On the mouth. One should circle it right there. One should place it on the forehead. At the heart. On the two shoulders. On the two thighs. Binding a garland. On the sense faculties. On the crown. On the face. Drawing it out from the mouth. One should circle it everywhere. One should circle it in the directions. Recollecting oneself and others as tathāgatas. Recollection from the heart. Placing it at the heart, one should generate joy. One should generate buddhahood itself. Meditating on being all the buddhas. One should place it on the five aggregates. One should spin it like a potter's wheel. Mindfulness of the body. One should place the tongue. One should long intensely with the mind. Recollection of generosity. Moral discipline. Loving-kindness. Diligence. Samādhi. Wisdom. Primordial wisdom. Power. Skillful means.

Definitive expression. Dhāraṇī. The grounds. Conduct. The discriminating knowledges. The unshared qualities. Dharma. Karma. Various bodies. The welfare of sentient beings. The buddhas too are the aim. Body. Speech. Mind. The Great Vajra.

Next, the speech mudrā of all the tathāgatas is the Dharma speech of the mudrā, endowed with a sound of very deep tone; by it the speech of all the tathāgatas is accomplished without obstruction. Then recitation is done through recollection. The same holds for accomplishment. Application to the rites is done by recollecting all the tathāgatas. This is the binding of the mudrās of all the tathāgatas.

Next, the binding of the mudrās of Vajra Sun Blazing like Fire. Here the great mudrā is conformity of one's whole being. The tantra of the samaya mudrā: binding the vajra bond inward and placing the thumb vajra within the blazing circle of all the fingers, one is able to burn, cook, and reduce to ashes the whole of the three realms, and the Blessed One too brings them under control. If, with that same mudrā, the left finger makes the threatening gesture and one summons with the hook of the right, everything is summoned.

The mudrā of Vajrahūṃkāra: with the vajra of the two wrathful vajra fists one binds the hook of the little fingers, and making the threatening gesture with it, one experiences all rites and all accomplishments. Binding the index fingers with the vajra palms, one should set the wrathful frown downward; swiftly radiating sparks and moving: this is the vajra frown. Burning everything, taming everything, and thoroughly subduing: this is the mudrā of the frown.

Turning the vajra palms well, the left index finger is the hook: it is effective for summoning, causing to enter, and all accomplishments. The vajra lotus with the middle fingers level is the treasury of the bud. The vajra lotus cuts through everything. The vajra bond: with a ring of thumb and index finger, the hūṃ of the right grasps the index finger; it summons all, causes them to enter, and is supremely accomplished. Arising from Vajrahūṃkāra: the right index finger grasps the syllable hūṃ of the left. This is called drawing the bowstring; it makes all impassioned and pierces them. From the vajra bond comes the supreme mudrā of Vajrahūṃkāra. In the vajra bond, the left vajra fist is fully grasped by the right, marked with a trident; it tears everything apart.

The little finger of Vajrahūṃkāra's right fist fully extended and placed upon the left hand is the raising of the khaṭvāṅga. It breaks open and tears apart the seals of all the malevolent ones, and all their implements for sealing, killing, eating, and so on are raised with the right hand, in the manner of holding a wheel, and cast away: it cuts everything and causes it to be cut. This is the wrathful vajra of the Great Vajra. Bringing down the right vajra and joining it: the tripatāka with petals radiating and moving. With the thumbs and the two index fingers of the vajra bond, the ring fingers arising from the vajra are joined level and the little fingers opened. It brings every glory, brings complete victory, crushes all the malevolent, and brings everything down.

The empowerment of Vajrasattva is explained.

For all of the vajra family,
making the vajra palms level,
hanging silk streamers with the two index fingers,
and circling the vajra of thumbs and ring fingers,
one confers empowerment.

The blazing five-pronged vajra should be received from the hand. Offering the master a fee with whatever one can afford, one will accomplish the perfect supreme of all accomplishments. So spoke the Blessed One Vajrasattva.

The vajra mudrā arises from Vajra ti rinti ri; it accomplishes everything unfailingly. Next, the four karma mudrās are the goddesses of the samaya mudrās.

Next, the karma samaya of the Blessed One: with the left vajra fist, the pride that accomplishes everything, and with the right vajra fist, brandishing the vajra. By this one accomplishes, for all, making faint, blazing, burning, falling, sealing, killing, raising up, victory over the three realms, firm application to everything stable and moving, accomplishments, and everything supreme.

Next, the karma mudrā: the vajra fist with the index finger in the threatening gesture is the wrathful vajra fist. Their action is the samaya mudrā. With the two index fingers one summons everything. With the other at the heart, and with the right, one cuts down all the malevolent. With the wrist knot, by hooking the right wrathful one, one summons all and makes them fully enter. Shooting the arrow with brandishing. Likewise, through the application of vajra dance of the vajra trident and the rest, one's own mudrā is cast upward. The karma samaya mudrā is cast upward. Making the karma samaya mudrā twofold, the karma mudrā of the vajra palms: the application of vajra dance, casting one's own samaya mudrā upward. Sealing, accomplishing, killing, bringing down, and reviving everything: this is the offering of vajra dance.

Next, the speech mudrā is the wrathful vajra speech, fierce and clear: the hūṃ that resounds like thunder from the clouds, the syllable hūṃ with its subtle sound. One should accomplish the aim of the practice.

Then, if it is not accomplished, one binds the karma mudrā. The practice is likewise to perform the great mudrā once. Firm application to the rites is done with the frown. It arises from Vajrahūṃkāra. The fierce fire offering of sesame oil mixed with mustard seed is done with the right hand. With that same one dispatches. With that same one binds everything. Immobility itself. Displaying sleep. The threatening finger of the wrathful vajra gaze. The mudrā is loosened. Joining the two index fingers well and then opening them. Binding a ring with those same fingers. Squeezing with the fangs. Then tearing apart. Striking with the right. Blazing with the two index fingers. The right index finger, with the left in the manner of falling. Opening the two index fingers as blazing. Cutting. With the two index fingers, cutting the tips of the joined fingers. Rubbing the two hands forcefully. Flinging all the fingers in every direction. Releasing the mudrā all at once. The threatening finger, straight and moving. The threatening finger, the right index finger moving like a serpent's hood. Holding the right index finger with the fangs. Displaying weeping, laughing, running, and the like. Snapping the fingers with the two index fingers. Placing the right close upon the left. Placing it firmly on the ground. One should move it. One should dance. Drawing out. One should bless. One should bless everywhere. Laughing "ha ha." One should summon by circling. One should draw. One should dispatch. Circling it all around, one should make it sink. Then casting away. Casting away swiftly. Likewise, drawing in from all sides. Likewise, drawing upward. One should roar oneself. One should weep. One should strike oneself. One should deliver blows to the heart. One should tear out one's own hair oneself. Binding into enmity. One should brandish one's own clothes. Wrathfully, one likewise displays blazing. The right index finger is the hook. With that same one causes to enter fully. The snap of the right. The snap of the left. Touching the tongue is done with the two index fingers. The hair on the limbs curls clockwise. Causing falling and the like. Meditating at the proper times. With the wrathful gaze, desiring with the mind at the foot of trees. Likewise on the ground. The navel. The eyebrows. The teeth. Blowing. Striking with the mudrā. Raising the victory banner. With a terrifying gaze joined with wrath. With an unsmiling gaze. Likewise, by binding the blazing. Circling the mudrā. One should place the index-finger vajra from the uṣṇīṣa. Likewise at the heart. Releasing that mudrā. Raising the mudrā to the side. Joining the tips of the index fingers firmly. By the gaze, falling from above to below. Drawing from below upward. The wrathful syllable hūṃ. With the terrifying gaze. Tearing with the vajra khaṭvāṅga, wrathfully. Meditating on the vajra at the heart. Tearing with the vajra trident. One should destroy the maṇḍala of the malevolent. With the syllable hūṃ. ha. Exhaling to the right. With that same, brandishing the coin. The wrathful gaze likewise. Pressing with the right fist and seizing the teeth with the left, one should summon. Brandishing the coin is done with both. Tearing the bell of the left. Likewise, the right fist of Vajrahūṃkāra is pressed by the vajra wrath of the left, and one should snatch with the fist whose little-finger ring is released. Tearing the bell of the right. Felling without effort is done by the mind. Tearing the wheel. Tearing the trident. The left of Vajrahūṃkāra is pressed by the right vajra fist, and likewise, releasing the ring, one should snatch. Recollecting the vajra, the bodhisattvas, all the tathāgatas, and the great Vajradhara, one should accomplish it before oneself.

This is the mudrā tantra of the king Vajrakrodha.

Next, the mudrā tantra of noble Avalokiteśvara. The great lotus: the vajra palms turned face down, the middle fingers, thumbs, and little fingers opened as four petals; it attains all dharmatā and makes all impassioned. The great mudrās are likewise. The samaya mudrā is the vajra lotus, as explained. They arise from the vajra bond of the lotus families. Among them, the lotus tathāgatas arise from the inner vajra bond. The mudrā named after a samādhi, like the lotus of the Tathāgata opened upward in the lap, is called the Lotus Eye. The vajra lotus of the great vajra lotus, binding the vajra at the heart, is called the Vajra Lotus Clinging to the Mountain. The index fingers of the lord of the great lotus made level, facing downward, and held in the lap: this is the mudrā called Lotus Tārā. The great lotus holding two jewel banners, and with it the giving of the lotus. These are called: those attained from all dharmatā, making all impassioned, delivering from all the masses of suffering of existence, and the perfect accomplishment that completely fulfills every intention. Tearing with the vajra trident.

Next, the mudrās of the lords of the lotus deities arise from the outer bond. Among them, that of Amogharāja: the two index fingers below, budded, are to be placed at the frown; this is the Lotus Frown Eye. The vajra bond facing upward and blazing is that of Nīlakaṇṭha. The left index finger bent and fully grasped by the thumb in the shape of a conch, the right thumb raised and lifted, the lotus budded with the little fingers level, and the wheel with the remaining fingers fully extended: this is the Lotus Vaiṣṇavī. The hook of the two index fingers bent, arising from the lotus, and the lotus with the remaining fingers opened, is called "arising from the lotus." That of Hayagrīva: the hook with the two index fingers tightly joined, the mouth of the mare stretched fully open, the tips of the ring fingers and the two little fingers fully extended and spread apart, the left moving and held: this is called the lotus hook. Through these, accomplishment in all empowerments, all rites, all liberations, and all summonings is unfailing.

The samādhi mudrā of the supreme body of the Tathāgata is explained.

Then, having bound the karma mudrā, the khaṭvāṅga, it is done by holding the samaya mudrās of the entire lotus family just as they are. Summoning the dhāraṇī through the samādhi of speech is knowing the meaning of recitation with the speech of the Dharma. Having bound the great lotus, one should perform the fire offering with the mind. With the mind one should pacify. One should protect the substances. One should purify. Thus one should accomplish it. This is the collection of mudrās for all rites. This is the tantra of the mudrās of noble Avalokiteśvara.

Then the great mudrā of Ākāśagarbha is likewise. The samaya mudrā is Vajraratna: the tips of the four digits, the thumbs and index fingers, are made even and drawn tightly downward. With the vajra palms joined for empowerment, one obtains all empowerments. With it one confers empowerment just as it should be. That same vajra is placed at the heart, facing upward. That same one, made into a fist: placing the fist at the crown of the head, one should present offerings of flowers and so on. Setting this same one as two, it is held as the karma mudrā. It is perfectly complete for obtaining the offering of the treasure of jewels of all empowerments. The karma samaya of the Blessed One brings clarity to full completion. Pressing the left fist at the heart, the right opens in the gesture of giving wealth. This is done by the jewel fist mudrā itself. The heart speech is recited by the nāga king, and he is commanded and likewise put to work in the rites. If it is not accomplished, Vajraratna, abiding in empowerment, issues commands to all from the sky. This is the collection of all the Dharma mudrās.

Unfailing, unobstructed accomplishment,
The accomplishing of the host of rites,
And the commands of the speech mudrā,
All swiftly arise from the sky.

So spoke the Blessed One Vajrasattva. This is the extensive ritual of all empowerments.

Then the outer tantra. The great mudrā is a bowl in the left hand and, in the right, an arrow aimed. Their samaya is made for entering. The thumbs and little fingers of the two hands are bound together like a chain of rings, and the index, middle, and ring fingers are made even, marked with three prongs: this is that of the brothers. Held just so, it is that of the goddesses. In the bound vajra, the tips of the four index and little fingers are joined to the four fingertips of the middle and ring fingers: this is the samaya that the goddesses find hard to transgress. It is the maṇḍala mudrā of the four sisters. When one is unable to carry out the examination of the maṇḍala, then if one binds this and looks, the examination, the seeing, and the worship of the maṇḍala will be accomplished as one wishes.

That of the lords of the hooded ones: binding the thumbs, the fingers facing outward are raised as a hood. This is the samaya for summoning, causing to enter, binding, and bringing under control all the nāgas; if they transgress it, they will die. The mahoraga hand accomplishes everything. By the method of tying the four middle and ring fingers well into a knot, they are raised and drawn together, and one should strike with the two tips of the two little fingers. When it is extended, all poisons blaze. When it is fully drawn in, all poisons are bound. By this same one, all poisons are eaten, transferred, and rendered harmless. A maṇḍala of the malevolent should be swiftly destroyed by the vajra held in the vajra fist, in wrath. This is the tantra of the outer mudrās.

Then the various mudrās of the Blessed One, the All-Supreme, the sublime Īśvara: placing the vajra palms in equipoise,

Three banners are fully held;
This is called Vajra Viṣṇu.
When released, it cuts down all.

With the vajra wheel mudrā, having cut off the heads of all the enemies that are the afflictions, all that is supreme is accomplished.

The little fingers joined with the thumbs,
The two hands above and below,
The three middle fingers marked as three prongs:
This accomplishes what is called Vajradharī.

The vajra samaya mudrā called Vajracaṇḍeśvarī is the unfailing samaya.

Placing vajra and lotus in equipoise,
Whatever is released, just as it is, so it becomes;
Even buddhahood, so very hard to gain,
Is accomplished by the recitation of the practice.

The mudrā called Vajra Dharmatā is Tārā.

The index fingers, bound, are hooked outward,
The thumbs covering the vajra:
It bestows the jewel of the Great Vajra.

This is the mudrā called Vajra Jewel.

Then this is the tantra of the mudrās of the assembly of all the accomplishments accomplished by the sublime Lord of All. There, first of all, the mudrā that accomplishes the first hundred accomplishments becomes the thousand great accomplishing mudrās. Just as they are, through the samaya mudrās and emblems, by the great vajra union of the right and left hands, they become the second thousand, which accomplishes the accomplishments of all rites; this is by binding the vajra bond inward.
